# Codex desktop context
- You are running inside the Codex (desktop) app, which allows some additional features not available in the CLI alone:

### Images/Visuals/Files
- In the app, the model can display images, videos, and audio using standard Markdown image syntax: ![alt](url)
- When an app or connector generates or edits media, prefer native media already displayed inline or a local output file already returned by the tool. For remote images, prefer Markdown image embeds when permitted by the app's URL-safety policy.
- For media that cannot be displayed directly, including remote video and audio, use the app's preview or display tool when available. Provide a Markdown link to a usable result URL only as a last resort if no preview or display tool can show the result.
- Do not download remote media to work around display restrictions.
- When sending or referencing a local image, video, or audio file, always use an absolute filesystem path in the Markdown image tag (e.g., ![alt](/absolute/path.png)); relative paths and plain text will not render the media.
- When a user asks to play an audio file, render it using Markdown image syntax with an absolute path (e.g., ![audio](/absolute/path.mp3)).
- When referencing code or workspace files in responses, always use full absolute file paths instead of relative paths.
- If a user asks about an image, or asks you to create an image, it is often a good idea to show the image to them in your response.
- Return web URLs as Markdown links (e.g., [label](https://example.com)).

### Pull request diff links
When referencing code from a GitHub PR, you can link directly to its diff in the app using:
[label](codex://review?pr=PR_URL&path=FILE_PATH&line=LINE&side=right)
URL-encode PR_URL and the repository-relative FILE_PATH. Use a verified one-based LINE from the current PR diff. Use side=left for the original code or side=right for the updated code. Enterprise links must use the hostname of this task's configured Git remote. Use ordinary file links for workspace code.

### Workspace Dependencies
- For sheets, slides, and documents, use the MCP server's `load_workspace_dependencies` tool (`mcp__codex_app__load_workspace_dependencies`) to find the bundled runtime and libraries.

### Automations
- This app supports recurring automations, reminders, monitors, follow-ups, and thread wakeups. When the user asks to create, view, update, delete, or ask about automations, search for the `automation_update` tool first, then follow its schema instead of writing raw automation directives by hand.
- For heartbeat monitors, preserve the user's notification intent in the saved prompt. Unless the user explicitly asks for periodic status updates, instruct the heartbeat to stay quiet while the monitored state is unchanged or non-actionable and to notify only on a meaningful change, completion, failure, or required user action. Do not add instructions such as "leave a brief status update" on every run.
- When an automation should archive a Codex thread on completion, use `set_thread_archived` instead of emitting raw archive directives.

### Thread Coordination
- Treat the terms "task", "thread", "chat", and "conversation" as synonyms when they clearly refer to conversations in Codex. Use "chat" when referring to conversations in the product. In technical discussions, preserve the terminology used by the code, APIs, logs, and documentation.
- When the user asks to create, fork, inspect, continue, hand off, pin, archive, unarchive, rename, or otherwise manage Codex threads, search for the relevant thread tool first: `create_thread`, `fork_thread`, `list_threads`, `list_archived_threads`, `read_thread`, `wait_threads`, `send_message_to_thread`, `handoff_thread`, `set_thread_archived`, or `set_thread_title`.
- When following another task's progress, prefer compact `wait_threads` snapshots over repeated `read_thread` calls. Use one target for single-task coordination and `timeoutMs: 0` for a compact immediate snapshot. `create_thread` dispatches asynchronously, so explicitly wait for progress. Use one bounded call for 1-8 targets with each target's `hostId` and cursor as `afterCursor`; it wakes on the first target that completes or needs attention, and timeout includes the latest commentary for all targets without waking on every commentary update. An up-to-date cursor suppresses already-delivered final text. Separate waits from one task may run serially. Do not narrate unchanged snapshots, and leave approval or user-input requests for the user.
- Only use `create_thread` when the user explicitly asks to create a new thread. Threads created this way are user-owned: they appear in the sidebar, and the user is expected to follow up with them directly. For subtasks of the current request, use multi-agent tools instead, including when the user explicitly asks for a subagent.
- After a successful `create_thread` call, emit `::created-thread{threadId="..."}` for a created thread or `::created-thread{clientThreadId="..."}` for queued worktree setup on its own line in your final response.

### Sidebar Organization
- Use `list_threads` to inspect pinned, custom, project, and task sidebar sections, and `list_projects` for project details. Use `create_sidebar_section`, `rename_sidebar_section`, `delete_sidebar_section`, `move_thread_to_sidebar_section`, `move_project_to_sidebar_section`, `reorder_sidebar_projects`, or `reorder_sidebar_sections` to organize tasks and projects. Moving an item into the pinned section pins it.

### Inline Code Comments
- Use the ::code-comment{...} directive when you need to attach feedback directly to specific code lines.
- Emit one directive per inline comment; emit none when there are no actionable inline comments.
- Required attributes: title (short label), body (one-paragraph explanation), file (path to the file).
- Optional attributes: start, end (1-based line numbers), priority (0-3).
- file should be an absolute path or include the workspace folder segment so it can be resolved relative to the workspace.
- Keep line ranges tight; end defaults to start.
- Example: ::code-comment{title="[P2] Off-by-one" body="Loop iterates past the end when length is 0." file="/path/to/foo.ts" start=10 end=11 priority=2}

### Inline Artifact Follow-Ups
- Format each artifact follow-up as an unescaped Markdown list item, `- :codex-followup[visible phrase]{prompt="Complete user request"}`; avoid closing brackets in the visible phrase and escape double quotes in the prompt.

### Git
- Branch prefix: `codex/`. Use this prefix by default when creating branches, but follow the user's request if they want a different prefix.

## User Profile

Joshua works on LSATU teaching, student records, curriculum quality, releases, and a Windows/AI-tool environment. He uses Codex for evidence-based implementation, independent review, connected-app drafting, recovery, and live-system checks. He values concept-first teaching, plain language for nontechnical workflows, and a hard distinction between source/static evidence and deployment or signed-in acceptance.

## User preferences

- Preserve strict scope: honor "Do not commit, push or publish," keep edits in the named path, and report deviations or blocked checks explicitly.
- Validate behavior, not just prose: make predictions, run broken and fixed cases, retain failure evidence/receipts, and distinguish direct proof from inference.
- Do not fabricate browser, device, deployment, persistence, permission, or acceptance evidence; keep tickets open until the requested paths are actually evidenced.
- For student lessons, teach the exact recorded sticking point first; use visual ordinary-language explanation before notation and preserve modality, complete conditions, and fact-versus-rule distinctions.
- When reusing student work, locate and assign the exact existing artifact rather than recreating or paraphrasing it; use a dry run and read-after-write verification for live assignments.
- Use the Codex in-app browser for LSAT Lab checks. Respect explicit approval gates; pilot approval does not authorize a later load.
- For shared Windows tools, diagnose before reinstalling and document availability for Codex, Claude Code, and Antigravity. Use separate PowerShell commands when requested: "ONE COMMAND PER CALL."
- For connected-app drafting, "Show the draft here; do not send, post, or modify anything." Match real demonstrated style.
- For research synthesis, retain only claims corroborated across two independent sources, each with an exact quote and source.

## General Tips

- Separate focused test/typecheck/build, deployment, and fresh signed-in acceptance; static approval is not visual, keyboard, interaction, or publication proof.
- Resume work by reconciling handoffs with current diffs, live configuration/database pulls, and `wiki/hot.md`; stale checkouts and status claims are recurring hazards.
- Database content writes: snapshot first, reconcile overlapping proposals, guard exact old text/MD5, use approved direct transport, then server-side audit. Do not hand-type huge SQL payloads.
- Windows `command not found` can be stale inherited PATH: check executable/WinGet/saved PATH, refresh or restart the app before reinstalling.
- Preserve unrelated edits, local conflicts, recovery data, secrets, and active browser/session state. GitHub parity is not laptop parity.

## developer

<multi_agent_role>You are `/root`, the primary agent in a team of agents collaborating to fulfill the user's goals.

At the start of your turn, you are the active agent.
You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents.
All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent without triggering a turn.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.
You can decide how much context you want to propagate to your sub-agents with the `fork_turns` parameter.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
They may be addressed as to=/root

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"`.</multi_agent_role>

## developer

<multi_agent_mode>Any earlier instruction enabling proactive multi-agent delegation no longer applies. Do not spawn sub-agents unless the user or applicable AGENTS.md/skill instructions explicitly ask for sub-agents, delegation, or parallel agent work.</multi_agent_mode>

# AGENTS.md instructions for C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Global rules — Joshua

These apply on **every** project on this machine, in every session. Project-level
`AGENTS.md` files add to this; they do not replace it.

Keep this file short. It reloads on every message.

---

## Who

Joshua Fisseha — `contact@aspiringattorneys.com`. Builds **LSAT U** (lsatprep.study) and
the Aspiring Attorneys brand. Teaches the LSAT: ~25 one-to-one sessions a week plus
consultations. Windows 11; a MacBook Pro runs some sessions too, so paths differ between
them — never assume `/Users/joshuaf/...` resolves here.

## Where things live

- **AI OS / second brain:** `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` — the Obsidian vault. `context/`,
  `wiki/`, `students/`, `corpus/`, `decisions/`, `brainstorms/`, `skills/`, `projects/`.
  Open this folder rather than starting with no folder, or memory cannot accumulate.
- **Skill catalog:** `dev/ai-os/skills/Codex` (179) and `skills/codex` (154), with
  `PARITY.md` declaring deliberate single-runtime skills and `INDEX.md` cataloging them.
- **LSAT U app:** repo `vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db` — React + Vite + TS + Tailwind +
  shadcn, Supabase backend, hosted on Lovable.
- **GitHub:** account `vnjkoxpl-42905` (auto-generated name, correct account).

## How to work with him

- **Do the whole task.** Deliver the finished thing, then say plainly what you skipped
  and why. Don't hand back a plan when the work was asked for.
- **Look before asking.** If the answer is on disk or reachable through a connected tool,
  go get it. He has Firecrawl, Google Workspace (`gws`), and `gh` — use them.
- **Say what's actually true.** Distinguish verified from assumed, every time. If a step
  failed or was skipped, lead with that. A confident wrong answer costs more than "I
  don't know yet."
- **Surface your own mistakes immediately**, including scope errors you notice mid-task.
- Absolute dates (`YYYY-MM-DD`), never "yesterday" or "last week".
- Don't re-ask what he already answered. Don't pad with options he didn't request.

- **Dictation fixes.** A message shaped like `word --> word` (any arrow, any spacing), or
  "save X in my vocabulary", is a Superwhisper correction. Invoke the `superwhisper` skill
  and apply it in that turn, whatever else is in progress.

## Writing as Joshua

Anything published under his name — course copy, lessons, emails, posts.

Plain, direct, confident. Short sentences. Teach the reasoning rather than asserting the
conclusion; this is a teaching brand and the reasoning *is* the product. Concrete beats
abstract. Never condescend to the reader.

**Never use:** "delve into", "dive deep", "unlock", "unleash", "harness the power of",
"in today's fast-paced world", "ever-evolving landscape", "it's not just X, it's Y",
"navigate the complexities", "at the end of the day", "elevate", "supercharge",
"game-changer", "seamless", "robust", "I hope this helps!", "Great question!",
"Certainly!". No em-dash pile-ups. No paragraph opening with "Moreover," /
"Furthermore," / "Additionally,". No emoji in professional writing unless asked.

Headers should say something, not label a category — "Why students miss this", not
"Analysis".

## Safety

- **Draft by default; send only when he says "send".** Email, messages, posts are drafted
  and shown to him. When he says "send it" (or equivalent) in chat, send it, then log it in
  `wiki/send-ledger.md`. Never send on your own initiative, and never treat a request to
  draft as a request to send.
- **Never delete** files, emails, or records without explicit confirmation.
- The Google Workspace grant is broad (it includes deleting all Drive files), so the
  restraint has to live in tool permissions, not in wording. A prompt is not a permission
  layer.
- Secrets stay in `.env` and the OS keyring. Never paste a key into a note, a commit, or
  a chat.

## Tools already connected

- **Firecrawl** — CLI + 28 skills. Prefer it over MCP; the hosted MCP server was removed
  on purpose (the key would sit in plaintext in `~/.Codex.json`).
- **Google Workspace** — `gws` CLI, GCP project `ai-os-joshua`. Gmail, Calendar, Drive,
  Docs, Sheets, Slides, Tasks, People.
- **GitHub** — `gh` CLI, authenticated.

## Cost discipline

`AGENTS.md` reloads on every message, so keep this file lean. Prefer CLIs to MCP servers
(an MCP server can cost ~18k tokens per message in tool definitions). `/clear` between
unrelated tasks. Run the `handoff` skill past ~25% context rather than waiting for
auto-compact.

---

*Written 2026-09-07 from observed behavior. Correct it whenever it's wrong — that is the
point of the file.*

## Shared Windows CLI tools

On Joshua's Windows laptop, `uv`, `yt-dlp`, `ffmpeg`, `ffprobe`, `ffplay`, and `deno` are shared Windows user installs. Read `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os\references\ai-workspace\windows-cli-toolchain.md` for their paths, versions, usage, and PATH refresh steps. Before reinstalling, check `Get-Command`, the saved user PATH, and the WinGet package. A running IDE may have stale PATH; refresh the shell or restart that IDE. Keep Python dependencies in project environments.

# Jay 2 — operating instructions for Codex (ai-os)

The canonical root is the repository directory containing this file (`C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` on this Windows machine). Resolve paths from the repository root instead of assuming a saved Mac or Windows absolute path. This is the router: it holds pointers, not knowledge. Read it, then follow the routing map to the right file instead of searching blind. Twin for Claude Code: `CLAUDE.md`.

## Identity and mission

You are **Jay 2**, Joshua's executive assistant. This root is his EA workspace and command center. Joshua is the founder of Aspiring Attorneys and builder of LSAT U (lsatprep.study), a premium LSAT prep platform; he is the product, voice, and content authority. Your job: take operations, admin, drafting, and research off his plate so his time goes to teaching and building. Frame work as decisions and tradeoffs, not basics.

Standing duties, run from here without re-briefing: student operations (transcripts, assignments, follow-ups via `students/`), prospect and consultation correspondence (drafted in his voice, **never auto-sent unless a pre-approved template**), platform actions on lsatprep.study, session-note ingestion from Gmail/Drive, and keeping the wiki ledgers (`tasks`/`hot`/`log`) current so the next session starts oriented.

## Routing map

Paths are relative to the repository root.

| Need | Path |
|---|---|
| **Who Joshua is** (identity, preferences, contact) | `context/about-me.md` |
| **The business** (offers, money, channels, gaps) | `context/work.md` |
| **Current priorities** (the 90-day slate) | `context/priorities.md` |
| **What we decided and why** (locked decisions) | `decisions/log.md` |
| Freshest session state (hot cache) | `wiki/hot.md` |
| LLM Wiki schema (source, page, ingest/query/lint rules) | `system/schema/llm-wiki.md` |
| Master index · work ledger · dated history | `wiki/index.md` · `wiki/tasks.md` · `wiki/log.md` |
| Product ground truth (pointers to live backends) | `wiki/dashboard.md` |
| LSAT teaching knowledge | `corpus/index.md` |
| A student — profile, transcripts, work, correspondence | `students/<name>/` — start at `students/README.md` |
| Voice for anything a student or prospect reads | `wiki/teaching-voice.md` |
| Was it sent? (every outbound draft/send) | `wiki/send-ledger.md` — append-only; Gmail is authority |
| Who the students are (name, email, sessions, links) | `students/roster.generated.md` — regenerate, never hand-edit |
| Learned facts and preferences (automemory) | native store `~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/` + `MEMORY.md` index |
| Skill / agent catalog | `skills/INDEX.md`, `agents/` |
| Systems Jay 2 can reach (registry) | `connections.md` — 7 data domains, mechanism/auth/freshness; read by `/audit` |
| Operator brain (how to think about automation) | `references/3ms-framework.md` — the Three Ms; walked weekly by `/level-up` |
| Automation build backlog (what to build next, ranked) | `references/automation-backlog.md` — corpus-derived gaps + tiers; feeds `/level-up` |
| How-it-works reference notes (methods, not state) | `references/` — also `references/claude-plus-codex.md`, `references/obsidian-command-center.md`, `references/second-brain-levels.md` |
| Web research and Firecrawl agents | `references/firecrawl.md` (guidelines in `references/firecrawl/`) |
| Shared Windows CLI tools for Codex, Claude Code, and Antigravity | `references/ai-workspace/windows-cli-toolchain.md` — installed paths, PATH refresh, and usage for `uv`, `yt-dlp`, FFmpeg, and Deno |
| Immutable source evidence · intake queue | `raw/sources/` · `raw/inbox/` — one inbox, one archive |
| Archived web clips, with provenance + hashes | `raw/sources/clips/<slug>/` — clip + `PROVENANCE.md` + `SHA256SUMS` |
| Raw personal-data exports (Google Takeout, Gemini session transcripts) | `raw/data-about-me/` — unprocessed PII, git-ignored; never read wholesale |
| Design/plan thinking, dated (grill-me + interview outputs) | `brainstorms/` — supersedes the retired `aios/` reform folder |
| Session handoffs · chat exports | `handoffs/` — operational records, deliberately OUTSIDE `raw/`; `session-handoff` writes here |
| Current local work in Claude/Codex cloud | `setup/github-sync/README.md`; private `codex/local-sync/<machine>/<checkout>` branches and generated `.cloud-sync/` metadata/conversation exports. Cloud edits use separate task branches. |
| External teardowns and research, dated | `research/` |
| Web-clipped articles (Obsidian Web Clipper) | `raw/inbox/` — intake queue; `ingest` copies and hash-verifies clips into `raw/sources/clips/`; processed originals stay until removal is authorized, so inbox presence alone does not mean unprocessed |
| Application code | `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` — own repo, own instructions, own remote |
| Other projects (life-path, …) | `../ai-os-projects/<name>/` — each with its own `CLAUDE.md` + `.claude/` |

Application repositories and dependencies stay outside this Obsidian vault, in sibling `../ai-os-projects/`. Do not create a junction from the vault back into that directory.

## Retrieval order

1. `wiki/hot.md` — current-session narrative and open threads.
2. `wiki/index.md` → the page (teaching knowledge: `corpus/index.md`; a student: their folder). Follow links; there is no semantic search and none is needed at this size.
3. The live system — the final freshness escalation.

**Live systems are authority for operational state; the brain mirrors them:** Cal.com + Google Calendar own sessions scheduled/used, LSAT U Supabase owns homework status/dossier/publish state, Stripe owns payment events, Gmail owns correspondence. On any disagreement, the backend wins. The brain is primary — nothing upstream holds them — for transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, curriculum, product specs, and decisions.

**Do not read the wiki unless the task needs it.** Retrieval costs context every turn. Skip it for: code edits in `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/`; shell/Git/file ops; questions the injected memories already answer; a skill run that names its own inputs; anything mechanical and self-contained. Route to it for: what is X · what did we decide and why · who a student is and where they stand · anything drafted in Joshua's voice for someone else · any decision touching roadmap, pedagogy, or UX.

**LLM Wiki schema:** For knowledge intake, knowledge questions, durable synthesis, and wiki maintenance, follow `system/schema/llm-wiki.md`. Use `ingest` as the single intake entry point; keep sources immutable, compile into existing pages, update the complete fenced catalog in `wiki/index.md`, and append changes to `wiki/log.md`. Preserve domain homes and live-system authority. Mechanical tasks retain the retrieval exceptions above.

**Wiki operations (LLM-wiki pattern):** the LLM writes/maintains `wiki/` and `corpus/`; Joshua sources, explores, asks. Raw sources are immutable — read, never modify. *Ingest:* read the source from `raw/`, discuss takeaways, write/update the pages it touches, update `wiki/index.md`, append to `wiki/log.md` (`## [YYYY-MM-DD] ingest | Title`); one source at a time, supervised, unless Joshua says batch. *Query:* index first, then pages; file valuable answers back as pages so explorations compound. *Lint:* `second-brain-lint` for contradictions, stale claims, orphans, missing pages.

## Voice

- **No em-dashes, ever.** Joshua never types them; a stray em-dash reads as AI and costs trust.
- **No AI-slop vocabulary** — never "delve," "tapestry," "testament," "demystify," "boasts," "in the realm of," and their kin.
- **Anything a student or prospect will read** routes through `wiki/teaching-voice.md` and is drafted, never sent, unless it is a pre-approved template.
- **LSAT teaching:** use project-owned `lsat-u-voice` and `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`; this Joshua-selected standard takes precedence over older vendor voice blends.
- **When an outbound message is authorized**, preserve the draft's paragraph breaks, list lines, and intentional spacing. After sending, compare the recipient-facing rendering with the draft and confirm exactly one copy landed. Report any mismatch; never resend automatically.
- **Lead with your recommendation, not a menu.** Be decisive; don't over-hedge.
- **Any prompt Joshua will paste elsewhere** goes inside a fenced code block, never inline prose.

## Tools, keys, and secrets

- **The entire secret model is `.env` + `.gitignore`.** The root `.env` (git-ignored — verify with `git check-ignore .env`) is Joshua's single central store for **everything**: API keys, passwords, and bank/account info (decided 2026-07-17). OAuth client secrets live in `~/.config/gws/`, outside the repo. There is no vault, no Keychain, no encrypted store (`records.sparsebundle` is dead) and none is wanted. Never paste a credential into chat — add it to `.env` directly in the editor.
- **Safety is the tool layer, not prompts:** allow/deny tool lists + scoped API keys. Jay 2 acts under its own credentials and never impersonates Joshua.
- **Connected live systems:** Cal.com, Google Calendar, LSAT U Supabase, Stripe, Gmail. Full registry in `connections.md`.
- **Google access is `gws` CLI only** (`gws gmail|drive|calendar|docs …`, keyring-auth; see `skills/codex/gws-shared/`). Never the Google connector MCPs — the CLI is faster, batchable, and exports Docs to markdown.
- **Shared Windows command tools:** use `uv`, `yt-dlp`, `ffmpeg`, `ffprobe`, `ffplay`, and `deno` from the Windows user PATH. For versions, locations, how to invoke them, or a stale terminal environment, read `references/ai-workspace/windows-cli-toolchain.md`. Check before reinstalling; restart the affected IDE when its inherited PATH is stale.
- **Government IDs stay outside this OS.** Everything else (student names, emails, schedules, transcripts, notes, payment *links*) is ordinary local working material — classify by content, never by filename.
- **Git remote is enabled.** `origin` is GitHub (`vnjkoxpl-42905/ai-os`, decided 2026-09-29). The outer repo never tracks `.env`, `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` (own repo), runtime logs, or locks. Do not create gitlinks.
- **New laptop restore:** GitHub contains tracked ai-os files and skills. It deliberately excludes `.env`, `raw/`, app repos, credentials, and machine sessions. Restore machine-specific files from the private backup using `setup/backup/README.md` and its `README - START HERE.md`; install tools separately and sign in again where sessions cannot be restored. Never move credentials, raw personal data, or active browser tokens into GitHub.
- **Authorized cloud transfer (2026-10-07):** private sync branches also carry portable skills, memory and conversation exports. Joshua explicitly authorized credential transfer; implementation uses the authenticated encrypted package described in `setup/github-sync/README.md`; plaintext secrets and its unlock key stay outside GitHub and Drive. Cloud authentication and native browser sessions still require separate verification.

## Skills

Everything is a skill — **there are no slash commands** (decided 2026-07-17: one kind, one home, one activation path). The catalog at `skills/` and `agents/` is inert storage; nothing auto-loads. An item runs only once **activated by copy** (not symlink) into `.agents/skills/` (Claude: `.claude/skills/`). Creating one writes to **both** the project and the catalog (`skills/{claude,codex}/`), project-scoped — never auto-global. `claude/` and `codex/` subtrees are never merged; a vendor item is a live symlink + a frozen `_snapshots/` copy. `system/scripts/check-skills.sh` validates the model; `skills/INDEX.md` indexes it.

Active in this root. Run `pickup` only when Joshua explicitly asks for it. Do not run it automatically at session start. Use `wrapup` at the end of substantive sessions:

| Skill | Use it when |
|---|---|
| `pickup` / `wrapup` | `pickup` only on Joshua's explicit request / `wrapup` at session end (wrapup persists to the wiki ledgers) |
| `session-handoff` | mid-work context reset near ~250k, or passing work Codex↔Claude — chat-only, writes nothing |
| `audit` | scoring the OS against the Four Cs (structural health, /100) |
| `level-up` | weekly 3Ms interview to find + ship one automation |
| `consultation-inquiry-drafts`, `-followup-drafts`, `inbound-tutoring-replies` | drafting to prospects/students — always via the teaching voice |
| `guided-drill-assignment` | assigning a student a drill |
| `lsat-u-platform-actions` | doing anything ON lsatprep.study from here (assign, post, message, ingest) |
| `tutorocean-profile-fill` | TutorOcean profile work |
| `prompt-architect-refiner` | refining a prompt before it ships |
| `accommodations-email` | "accommodations email to X" (dictation: "accommodations scale"); pre-approved send, QP form attached, self-logging |

When a skill run exposes a gap, improve the skill before moving on. Runtime-specific catalog differences must be justified in `skills/PARITY.md`; undeclared differences are defects.

## Guardrails

- **95% rule.** Make no changes until you are 95% confident what Joshua wants. Ask until you are.
- **Lovable credits require confirmation.** Never take an action that spends or may spend Lovable credits without Joshua's explicit confirmation immediately before the action. Do not infer approval from a broader request to fix, build, deploy, or continue. Free Lovable controls and direct deployment paths may be used without this credit-spend confirmation.
- **Lovable Cloud deploys through Lovable.** LSAT U currently uses a Lovable Cloud-managed backend. Deploy its Edge Functions through the Lovable agent, not the Supabase CLI, unless the backend has first been migrated to a separately managed Supabase project. Confirm this route with Joshua before starting a Lovable deployment.
- **Root stays thin.** Don't accumulate files at the root; work products go to their homes (wiki, students, corpus, projects). Anything left at root gets filed before the session ends.
- **Delegate bulk work.** Exploration or analysis spanning 3+ files goes to a sub-agent that returns summaries, not file dumps.
- **Shell ergonomics.** Run one command per call — chained `cmd; cmd` and `rm -rf X && clone Y` forms trip permission denials that the split commands would not. Never poll with `sleep`. Use backgrounded execution for anything slow, and raise the timeout explicitly for known-slow ops (large moves, typecheck-gated pushes, full verify runs) rather than eating the default.
- **Search before creating.** One authoritative page per subject — update it, don't grow a parallel truth. `wiki/index.md` catalogs every page and updates on every ingest.
- **Preserve provenance and source hashes.** Never move, delete, or modify a migration source root — copy and hash-verify. Never follow a source symlink outside its registered root.
- **Conflicts stay explicit until adjudicated** — never silently overwrite one source generation with another.
- **Automation writes only generated files or fenced generated sections,** through compare-and-swap.
- **Obey nested instruction files** — they win over broader ones for their subtree. LSATU's recovered `CLAUDE.md`/`AGENTS.md` are inviolate: never regenerate them without Joshua's explicit approval.
- **Memory = native automemory.** "Remember that…" writes one fact per file to the native store; `MEMORY.md` indexes it. This file is the rules; memory is learned facts — never mix them. Transcripts and raw captures never auto-promote.
- **Append meaningful system changes** to `wiki/log.md`; log locked decisions to `decisions/log.md`.

## Dual-runtime (Codex + Claude)

- Begin at the Git-root `AGENTS.md` and follow directory-scoped instruction files toward the working path; the nearest file wins for its subtree.
- Keep root instructions inside the runtime byte budget. Move narrow rules to the narrowest owning directory rather than growing this file.
- Activated skills are **copies** in `.agents/skills/` (from `skills/codex/`); agent definitions convert to `.codex/agents/*.toml` with the instructions in `developer_instructions`.
- Claude reads `CLAUDE.md` and never this file. Anything that must hold for both runtimes goes in BOTH files — when you change it here, mirror it there; keep the routing map and guardrails byte-identical between them.
- Activation is unequal by design: Codex reads copies in `.agents/skills/`, Claude reads copies in `.claude/skills/`. Codex and Claude both activate the LLM Wiki ingest, query, and lint workflows. Other runtime-specific activation differences remain declared in `skills/PARITY.md`. Never have separate runtimes writing the same page concurrently.
- Never have Claude and Codex editing the same file at once; pass work between them by session handoff (summarize → clear → paste). At ~250k tokens, hand off rather than riding autocompact.

</INSTRUCTIONS>

Returns projectId when creation starts; call render_project_widget with that projectId to show build progress. Projects use Lovable\'s backend default stack and are full-stack TypeScript apps with Tailwind + shadcn/ui. To start the project from a design system (components, tokens, styles), pass `design_systems` with an ID from `list_design_systems`. [Task 2]\\n"}\n{"chunk_id":"84b6aa","wall_time_seconds":0.3011835,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1681,"output":"---\\r\\nname: lsat-u-platform-actions\\r\\ndescription: Operate LSAT U from AI OS. Assign complete interactive Classroom lessons directly through the local lsat-u-lessons MCP server, verify the saved content and student visibility headlessly, and log the assignment. Use the Node CLI for other supported actions.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nFor drills, messages, student data, access, signups, due dates, unassign, and unsend, use `assign-lsatu-work`. On Windows, invoke its Node entry point directly instead of relying on Bash being on PATH.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT U Platform Actions\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s workplace is this brain workspace. LSAT U operations should run headlessly when a supported tool exists. The browser is for checking what a person sees.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Mechanism\\r\\n\\r\\nFor an interactive HTML lesson, call the `lsat-u-lessons` MCP tool `publish_interactive_lesson` with the student\'s exact email, absolute HTML path, and title. The local stdio server is `system/scripts/lsatu-lesson-mcp.mjs`; Codex registration is `lsat-u-lessons`. It calls `system/scripts/lsatu-admin.mjs post-lesson`, which authenticates with the existing admin credentials, posts through `mcp_post_classroom_note`, verifies the saved HTML exactly and student visibility, and then archives only earlier versions with the same lesson marker. A repeat of the current version returns the same item without another notification. Use `dry_run: true` when the student or replacement is uncertain; otherwise publish in one call. MCP tools newly registered during a session may only appear in a new Codex session; the direct Node command is the immediate fallback.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML must be a complete document inside AI OS, under 500 KB, with `<meta name=\\"lsatu-interactive-lesson\\" content=\\"unique-lesson-id\\">`. Reuse the same lesson ID for revisions of that lesson; use a different ID for a different lesson. Inline the CSS, JavaScript, fonts, and visual assets. Preflight rejects nonembedded resources; do not bypass it or paste HTML into an ordinary sanitized note. The already-live Classroom renderer loads marked HTML in a sandboxed iframe with scripts and forms enabled. **Daily lesson posting is a content operation and never requires Lovable.** Only an actual app renderer change follows the separate app deployment rules.\\r\\n\\r\\nRoutine verification is headless: the publisher reads the new item back, compares its HTML byte for byte, checks that it is visible to the intended student, and confirms one active version. The result includes its route and SHA-256. Use Codex\'s in-app browser only when a visual or interaction defect is suspected or the page design or renderer changed. A teacher login cannot open another student\'s `/classroom/item/<id>` route directly. Do not claim a student-session visual check unless one was actually performed.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe student opens the full lesson from Classroom **Notes and recaps**, the in-app notification, or its Inbox link. Classroom Home may show a recent note as a “Read” row, but it has a four-row limit shared with urgent work, so do not promise that every assigned note stays on Home. This does not affect the note\'s visibility in Notes and recaps or its direct item route.\\r\\n\\r\\n## UI fallback routing\\r\\n\\r\\nThe current student verification route is `/teacher/students/<student-id>/notes`. Its “Posted to their Classroom” section shows active notes and labels archived notes as hidden from the student. Use the browser\'s normal controls for any unsupported action, then verify the saved state through a fresh read. In Codex, use the in-app browser, not Chrome. Do not rely on old `/teacher?ws=` routes or old browser-tool names.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Order of operations (every action)\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Identify the student** exactly (roster names include email; ambiguity → one\\r\\n   concise clarifying question). Check `students/<name>/` for context and\\r\\n   `assignment-log.md` to avoid repeats.\\r\\n2. **Do the action** through MCP or the headless Node CLI when supported. Use the UI fallback only for unsupported actions.\\r\\n3. **Verify it landed**: use the publisher\'s read-after-write result to confirm exact HTML, student visibility, and one active copy of this lesson ID. It archives earlier versions of that ID and leaves unrelated same-title notes alone. Check notifications where available. If verification fails, inspect current items before retrying; never blindly post twice.\\r\\n4. **Log it**: append to `students/<name>/assignment-log.md` (create if missing) with\\r\\n   date, action, item, and evidence; drill verifications also go to\\r\\n   `wiki/guided-drill-verification-log.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Guardrails\\r\\n\\r\\n- Every action here hits **real students on prod**. Anything a student will see\\r\\n  (message text, homework instructions, note bodies) is written in Joshua\'s voice —\\r\\n  route through `wiki/teaching-voice.md`.\\r\\n- If Joshua\'s request fully specifies the action (\\"assign Lydia the conditional\\r\\n  drill\\"), execute without re-asking. If content is open-ended (a message body he\\r\\n  hasn\'t dictated), draft it and show him before sending.\\r\\n- Fail closed: if verification is ambiguous, say exactly what was and wasn\'t\\r\\n  confirmed. Never report an action complete on the strength of a click alone.\\r\\n- No student-facing action as a side effect of a different request.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Paid session credits\\r\\n\\r\\nUse `node system/scripts/lsatu-admin.mjs passes <exact-email>` for balances and `pass-detail` for evidence. An exhausted pass can be replaced with `pass-create <exact-email> --credits N --minutes 70`; dry-run first, then verify through a fresh read. Preserve exhausted passes.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore manually restoring a Stripe package, inspect its `stripe_payments` row and the webhook delivery response. The 2026-09-27 policy returned `grant: awaiting_teacher` for intentional teacher approval. On 2026-10-02, Joshua approved and deployed automatic crediting for new validated, confidently matched package Checkouts, including delayed successes. Existing rows default `auto_grant_eligible=false`; historical, dismissed, and granted payments are not automatically recredited. Unmatched payments stay in review. Verify the live row and deployment before diagnosing a bug; an empty `booking_commerce_events` read is not proof of no payment. Evidence: `research/2026-10-02-celia-payment-crediting.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\nAssociate any manual restoration with its exact Stripe payment/event in the booking pass notes. Reconcile the pending teacher grant prompt without adding more credits, using the supported payment-ledger operation after checking its semantics. Do not replay the webhook or invoke `admin_grant_stripe_payment` after manual crediting; either can duplicate sessions. A dismissed payment is not marked granted, and future reconciliation must skip it or explicitly honor the manual fulfillment.\\r\\n"}'}]

But that is the very thing we are trying to define.\\\\n\\\\nSo given that the definition sounds tidy, how could we argue it still fails to settle what is included? Show that we cannot agree on who the medieval epistemologists were. Then the test has nothing to run on.\\\\n\\\\nNotice what is NOT a problem for the historian: disagreement. The historian already says that if one epistemologist believed X and another believed the opposite, both claims count. Contradictions among the epistemologists were built into the plan.\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"7d3ea25e-dd71-4ada-937c-ae04e2252a50\\", \\"position\\": 3, \\"label\\": \\"Goal\\", \\"body\\": \\"Look for an answer that makes it hard to identify who the medieval epistemologists were. If we can\'t pick them out, the definition can\'t tell us what is included.\\\\n\\\\nWatch out for answers about epistemologists contradicting each other. The historian built that into the plan, so it doesn\'t weaken it.\\"}], \\"notes\\": [{\\"row_id\\": \\"3f5c68c4-8737-490f-b41b-1ca69027a118\\", \\"letter\\": \\"A\\", \\"body\\": \\"Overlap with ancient thinkers doesn\'t stop the test from running. Ask the historian\'s question about a shared belief: did any medieval epistemologist believe it? Yes, so it is in. Where else the belief came from has no effect on whether the definition can sort claims.\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"01678eae-d0c8-4317-8851-790c8b1813a1\\", \\"letter\\": \\"B\\", \\"body\\": \\"This is about why they held their epistemological beliefs, not which beliefs they held. The definition only asks whether a medieval epistemologist believed a claim. A belief shaped by \\\\\\"nonepistemological matters\\\\\\" is still one of their epistemological beliefs, and the test still sorts it.\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"2ab8be91-6574-4c5d-b0dc-d80bbe02a89e\\", \\"letter\\": \\"C\\", \\"body\\": \\"The definition only collects their \\\\\\"epistemological beliefs,\\\\\\" and the historian only asks about \\\\\\"some epistemological claim.\\\\\\" Passages that are \\\\\\"clearly not about epistemology\\\\\\" are simply left out. Mixed writings make the reading longer, but they don\'t stop the test from deciding what is included.\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"28029a1b-b551-46c4-93ea-97824d1d5775\\", \\"letter\\": \\"D\\", \\"body\\": \\"This is the trap the stimulus warned us about. The historian says that if any medieval epistemologists believed the opposite of a claim, that opposite claim also counts. Contradictions were planned for, so this answer describes something the definition already handles. It doesn\'t make the definition any harder to apply.\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"6ed8926a-96f2-4261-831b-8f6ed4b064c7\\", \\"letter\\": \\"E\\", \\"body\\": \\"The definition sorts claims by asking whether \\\\\\"any medieval epistemologists\\\\\\" believed them. Even if every belief they held was borrowed from antiquity, the definition still functions — you would identify the medieval epistemologist…1971 tokens truncated…llen from a drug-producing crop drifts into a nearby field of ordinary crops of the same species, the pollen could fertilize that ordinary crop and turn it into a drug-producing crop.\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"d1c38a19-c55c-4bbc-99be-1c6bb16adb74\\", \\"position\\": 2, \\"label\\": \\"Evaluate\\", \\"body\\": \\"The author seems to be making a couple assumptions:\\\\n1. She thinks that crops being genetically engineered to produce important drugs are the drugs themselves.\\\\nIs that right? I thought maybe scientists were bioengineering a crop that has a certain structure or molecule that\'s used in combination with other ingredients to make the finished product drug, but the plant might not itself be a drug.\\\\nMaybe they\'re bioengineering a special type of wheat crop that has an enzyme that works well with a chemotherapy pill, so the combined drug will be easier on people\'s stomachs.\\\\nWhile the genetically engineered version of the plant may reach our food supply, will the drug reach our food supply?\\\\n2. Is it really a plausible concern that a \\\\\\"drug\\\\\\" plant would cross-pollinate with a non-drug plant and sneak into the food supply?\\\\nFirst of all, are there any non-drug crop X\'s being grown close enough to a drug crop X for this pollen story to happen? Maybe they don\'t grow the drug version and the regular version anywhere near each other.\\\\nMaybe the drug versions are all grown in greenhouses, where the wind would have no ability to spread the pollen.\\\\nLet\'s say the pollen is spread, do we need to worry that the GMO version would overtake the normal version? What if the GMO stuff is recessive and the normal stuff is dominant genetics. It might be that even if pollen from drug plants blew over to normal plants, the normal plants would win out.\\\\nLet\'s say the pollen is spread, and the drug version of the crop does overtake the regular crops, do we need to worry that those crops will enter the food supply?\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"28c5b825-0584-4296-ba19-94bc4225d797\\", \\"position\\": 3, \\"label\\": \\"Goal\\", \\"body\\": \\"Look for an answer making it seem like we have less to be worried about, whether that\'s because the plant isn\'t \\\\\\"a drug\\\\\\" in and of itself, or because the storyline of it accidentally pollinating and overtaking regular plants before sneaking into our food supply sounds far fetched.\\"}], \\"old_notes\\": [{\\"row_id\\": \\"3ae82b06-98b5-4ef7-a819-629993fad1a7\\", \\"letter\\": \\"A\\", \\"body\\": \\"This answer addresses the severity of the consequence rather than the likelihood of it occurring. The argument claims drugs could contaminate the food supply through pollen drift. Saying the drugs are not harmful does not challenge whether contamination happens — it just says the contamination would not be dangerous. But the argument\'s concern is about unintended pharmaceutical contamination of the food supply as a matter of principle and safety. Conceding that contamination occurs while arguing it is harmless does not weaken the claim that drugs will \\\\\\"end up in the general food supply.\\\\\\" A strong weakener must challenge the mechanism, not downplay the consequences.\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"3ca3bfff-6aa0-45d5-b8e4-5dc7bf7e6f7c\\", \\"letter\\": \\"B\\", \\"body\\": \\"This answer says that if pollen drift were prevented, there would be no risk of contamination. But this actually confirms the argument\'s core claim — that pollen drift IS the mechanism by which drugs could enter the food supply. Saying \\\\\\"you can prevent the risk by preventing drift\\\\\\" validates the causal chain rather than breaking it. It is an operational suggestion for risk management, not a logical challenge to the argument\'s reasoning. The argument would still be correct that the risk exists; this answer just says the risk could be mitigated through active prevention measures.\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"bf754625-bf1f-488f-afde-ff800c3dcbac\\", \\"letter\\": \\"C\\", \\"body\\": \\"This answer says the plants engineered for drug production are not among those constituting the largest portion of the food supply. This limits the potential scope of contamination to minor food crops — pollen would only affect closely related species, and if the drug crops are not closely related to major staples, the biggest crops might be safe. However, this does not eliminate the risk. Cross-pollination could still affect some food crops, even minor ones. \\\\\\"Minor\\\\\\" food crops still enter the food supply; they are just consumed in smaller quantities. The argument\'s concern is that drugs could contaminate any food crops, and narrowing the scope does not break the causal mechanism.\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"a289da90-b50c-4d22-b9cf-e6c42994d2b3\\", \\"letter\\": \\"D\\", \\"body\\": \\"This answer breaks the causal chain at its most critical link. Even if pollen drifts from drug-producing crops to ordinary food crops of the same species, and even if cross-pollination transfers the drug-producing genetic trait, the drugs are not present in the parts of the plant that people actually eat. The genetic modification may be inherited, and the plant may technically produce the pharmaceutical compound, but if that compound appears only in roots, stems, or leaves of a crop harvested for its fruit or grain, it never enters the food supply. The mechanism exists, but it does not reach your plate. The argument\'s conclusion — that drugs will end up in the food people consume — does not follow.\\"}, {\\"row_id\\": \\"88958a04-d640-4eb8-8590-63e0d3cf36b2\\", \\"letter\\": \\"E\\", \\"body\\": \\"This answer says scientists could identify which food crops have been cross-pollinated with drug-producing crops. If scientists can identify contaminated crops, that implicitly concedes contamination happens — the ability to detect it actually supports the argument\'s premise that cross-pollination occurs. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 6.3 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"content":[{"type":"text","text":"{\\n  \\"rows\\": [\\n    {\\n      \\"current_database\\": \\"postgres\\",\\n      \\"supabase_url\\": null\\n    }\\n  ]\\n}"}],"structuredContent":{"rows":[{"current_database":"postgres","supabase_url":null}]},"isError":false}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"a881c7","wall_time_seconds":0.3631715,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":919,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: operating-layer\\r\\nsubject: Lovable Cloud (LSAT U backend platform)\\r\\nupdated: 2026-07-19\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Lovable Cloud (LSAT U backend platform)\\r\\n\\r\\nSource: [Lovable Cloud docs clipping](<../raw/sources/clips/lovable-cloud/Lovable Cloud.md>) (`raw/sources/clips/lovable-cloud/`) (docs.lovable.dev/integrations/cloud, ingested 2026-07-19). This is what runs the LSAT U production backend (`atrcqxpmqrynmtvvyjju.supabase.co`).\\r\\n\\r\\n## What it is\\r\\n\\r\\nLovable Cloud is Lovable\'s managed backend: a Supabase-based stack (Postgres, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, scheduled Jobs, AI gateway) that Lovable itself provisions and operates. The project is **not** in Joshua\'s own Supabase org and has no supabase.com dashboard — everything is managed through the Lovable editor\'s Cloud tab or by prompting Lovable in chat.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Capabilities that matter for LSAT U\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Database**: schema changes via SQL migrations that *Lovable* applies (pushed migrations do not auto-apply — confirmed 2026-07-19 when two booking migrations sat unapplied while edge functions synced). Daily backups, ~14 days retained, restorable from Cloud tab (restore is destructive/permanent).\\r\\n- **Storage**: private-by-default buckets, files up to 2 GB. Public buckets blocked by default (workspace setting). Files are NOT included in DB exports — downloaded separately from Cloud tab → Storage.\\r\\n- **Edge Functions**: deployed automatically on git push (this part does auto-sync). Logs visible in Cloud tab.\\r\\n- **Jobs**: cron-style scheduled tasks, created/changed via SQL or Lovable chat.\\r\\n- **Auth**: email/phone/Google/Apple; settings managed in Cloud tab.\\r\\n- **Secrets**: write-only store for backend keys, injected into edge functions. `SUPABASE_*` and `LOVABLE_*` are reserved/auto-managed; the service-role key exists in the edge-function env (`Deno.env.get(\\"SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY\\")`) but is never shown to the user.\\r\\n- **`.env` rule**: `VITE_*` values live in the committed `.env` (must NOT be gitignored in the lsatu repo — breaks Lovable previews); backend secrets live in the Secrets UI, never in `.env`.\\r\\n- **Ops via chat**: health checks, slow-query analysis + auto-indexing, instance resize (Tiny→Large with approval card).\\r\\n- **Billing**: one credit balance; Cloud + AI usage = Run credits; 20-credit monthly Cloud grant + 4-credit AI grant on paid plans. Auto-pause on inactivity (scheduled jobs prevent auto-pause).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Control boundaries (Jay 2 vs Lovable)\\r\\n\\r\\nJay 2 can reach the backend only through: the deployed edge functions (admin JWT via `LSATPREP_ADMIN_*` creds), the public REST/auth APIs (anon key), and the git repo (code + migrations that Lovable then deploys/applies).\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lovable-UI-only** (no API path, but Joshua can drive manually): the **Cloud tab → SQL editor** runs arbitrary SQL including DDL — discovered 2026-07-19 — so pending migrations never actually block on Lovable chat: Jay 2 writes the SQL, Joshua pastes and Runs. Still UI-only with no external path: secrets management, storage browsing/download, backups/restore, instance sizing, auth provider config, pause/remove, DB exports. There is no service-role key or DB connection string available outside Lovable, by design.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Escape hatches**: full DB export (≤5 GB, 1/day) + GitHub repo = migration path to a self-owned Supabase project, but Lovable offers no one-click transfer, storage/auth/secrets must be rebuilt by hand, and Cloud→Supabase switching of a live project is explicitly unsupported. Decision 2026-07-19: stay on Lovable Cloud; treat Lovable chat as the operator for DB-level actions (\\"apply the pending migrations\\", \\"run a health check\\").\\r\\n"}'}]

'{"chunk_id":"4158fa","wall_time_seconds":0.2938903,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1436,"output":"\\"\\"\\"Build guarded SQL for step 3.\\r\\nUsage: python -I apply3.py <out_prefix> <proposals.jsonl> <verdicts.jsonl> [more pairs...]\\r\\nResolves each rewrite to its final steps/notes (proposal, or verifier\'s fix), runs hard gates,\\r\\nthen writes guarded UPDATEs (old body must still match) and guarded INSERTs, plus <prefix>-final.json (old kept as snapshot).\\"\\"\\"\\r\\nimport json, sys, re, os, hashlib\\r\\nmd5 = lambda s: hashlib.md5(s.encode(\'utf-8\')).hexdigest()\\r\\nsys.stdout.reconfigure(encoding=\'utf-8\')\\r\\nHERE = os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))\\r\\nGENDER = re.compile(r\\"\\\\b(he|she|him|her|his|hers|himself|herself)\\\\b\\", re.I)\\r\\nprefix = sys.argv[1]\\r\\npairs = [(sys.argv[i], sys.argv[i + 1]) for i in range(2, len(sys.argv), 2)]\\r\\nq = lambda s: \\"\'\\" + s.replace(\\"\'\\", \\"\'\'\\") + \\"\'\\"\\r\\nfinal, held = [], []\\r\\nfor pf, vf in pairs:\\r\\n    props = [json.loads(l) for l in open(pf, encoding=\'utf-8\') if l.strip()]\\r\\n    verd = {}\\r\\n    for l in open(vf, encoding=\'utf-8\'):\\r\\n        if l.strip():\\r\\n            v = json.loads(l); verd[v[\'qid\']] = v\\r\\n    for p in props:\\r\\n        if p.get(\'action\') != \'rewrite\': continue\\r\\n        v = verd.get(p[\'qid\'])\\r\\n        if not v: held.append((p[\'qid\'], \'no verdict\')); continue\\r\\n        if v[\'verdict\'] not in (\'approve\', \'fix\'): held.append((p[\'qid\'], v[\'verdict\'] + \': \' + v.get(\'note\', \'\'))); continue\\r\\n        steps, notes = (v[\'new\'][\'steps\'], v[\'new\'][\'notes\']) if v[\'verdict\'] == \'fix\' else (p[\'steps\'], p[\'notes\'])\\r\\n        pk = json.load(open(f\\"{HERE}/packets/{p[\'qid\']}.json\\", encoding=\'utf-8\'))\\r\\n        old_steps = {s[\'row_id\']: s for s in pk[\'steps\']}\\r\\n        old_notes = {n[\'row_id\']: n for n in pk[\'notes\']}\\r\\n        text = \' \'.join([s[\'body\'] + \' \' + (s[\'label\'] or \'\') for s in steps] + [n[\'body\'] for n in notes])\\r\\n        old_text = \' \'.join([s[\'body\'] + \' \' + (s[\'label\'] or \'\') for s in pk[\'steps\']] + [n[\'body\'] for n in pk[\'notes\']])\\r\\n        err = None\\r\\n        if \'—\' in text or \'–\' in text: err = \'dash\'\\r\\n        elif len(GENDER.findall(text)) > len(GENDER.findall(old_text)): err = \'pronoun\'\\r\\n        elif \'logic game\' in text.lower(): err = \'logic games\'\\r\\n        elif sorted(n[\'letter\'] for n in notes) != list(\'ABCDE\'): err = \'notes not A-E\'\\r\\n        elif any(not n[\'body\'].strip() for n in notes) or any(not s[\'body\'].strip() for s in steps): err = \'empty body\'\\r\\n        elif set(old_steps) - {s[\'row_id\'] for s in steps}: err = \'dropped a step row\'\\r\\n        elif any(s[\'row_id\'] and s[\'row_id\'] not in old_steps for s in steps) or any(n[\'row_id\'] and n[\'row_id\'] not in old_notes for n in notes): err = \'unknown row id\'\\r\\n        if err: held.append((p[\'qid\'], \'gate: \' + err)); continue\\r\\n        final.append({\'qid\': p[\'qid\'], \'explanation_id\': pk[\'explanation_id\'], \'steps\': steps, \'notes\': notes, \'old_steps\': pk[\'steps\'], \'old_notes\': pk[\'notes\']})\\r\\nprint(\'final\', len(final), \'held\', len(held))\\r\\nfor h in held: print(\'  HELD\', h)\\r\\njson.dump(final, open(prefix + \'-final.json\', \'w\', encoding=\'utf-8\'), ensure_ascii=False)\\r\\nupd_steps, upd_notes, ins_steps, ins_notes = [], [], [], []\\r\\nfor f in final:\\r\\n    os_ = {s[\'row_id\']: s for s in f[\'old_steps\']}; on = {n[\'row_id\']: n for n in f[\'old_notes\']}\\r\\n    for s in f[\'steps\']:\\r\\n        if s[\'row_id\']:\\r\\n            o = os_[s[\'row_id\']]\\r\\n            if o[\'body\'] != s[\'body\'] or (o[\'label\'] or \'\') != (s[\'label\'] or \'\'): upd_steps.append((s[\'row_id\'], o[\'body\'], s[\'body\'], o[\'label\'] or \'\', s[\'label\'] or \'\'))\\r\\n        else: ins_steps.append((f[\'explanation_id\'], s[\'position\'], s[\'label\'] or \'\', s[\'body\']))\\r\\n    for n in f[\'notes\']:\\r\\n        if n[\'row_id\']:\\r\\n            o = on[n[\'row_id\']]\\r\\n            if o[\'body\'] != n[\'body\']: upd_notes.append((n[\'row_id\'], o[\'body\'], n[\'body\']))\\r\\n        else: ins_notes.append((f[\'explanation_id\'], n[\'letter\'], n[\'body\']))\\r\\nprint(\'updates: steps\', len(upd_steps), \'notes\', len(upd_notes), \'| inserts: steps\', len(ins_steps), \'notes\', len(ins_notes))\\r\\nfiles = []\\r\\ndef chunks(rows, size_of):\\r\\n    cur, sz = [], 0\\r\\n    for r in rows:\\r\\n        s = size_of(r)\\r\\n        if cur and sz + s > 50000: yield cur; cur, sz = [], 0\\r\\n        cur.append(r); sz += s\\r\\n    if cur: yield cur\\r\\nfor rows in chunks(upd_steps, lambda r: len(r[2]) + 200):\\r\\n    vals = \',\\\\n\'.join(f\\"({q(r[0])}::uuid,{q(md5(r[1]))},{q(r[2])},{q(r[3])},{q(r[4])})\\" for r in rows)\\r\\n    files.append(f\\"update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl=\'\' then t.label else v.nl end from (values\\\\n{vals}\\\\n) v(id,ob,nb,ol,nl) where t.id=v.id and md5(t.body)=v.ob returning t.id;\\")\\r\\nfor rows in chunks(upd_notes, lambda r: len(r[2]) + 100):\\r\\n    vals = \',\\\\n\'.join(f\\"({q(r[0])}::uuid,{q(md5(r[1]))},{q(r[2])})\\" for r in rows)\\r\\n    files.append(f\\"update lr_explanation_choice_notes t set body=v.nb from (values\\\\n{vals}\\\\n) v(id,ob,nb) where t.id=v.id and md5(t.body)=v.ob returning t.id;\\")\\r\\nfor rows in chunks(ins_steps, lambda r: len(r[3]) + 200):\\r\\n    vals = \',\\\\n\'.join(f\\"({q(r[0])}::uuid,{r[1]},{q(r[2])},{q(r[3])})\\" for r in rows)\\r\\n    files.append(f\\"insert into lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id,position,label,body) select v.e,v.p,v.l,v.b from (values\\\\n{vals}\\\\n) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;\\")\\r\\nfor rows in chunks(ins_notes, lambda r: len(r[2]) + 150):\\r\\n    vals = \',\\\\n\'.join(f\\"({q(r[0])}::uuid,{q(r[1])},{q(r[2])})\\" for r in rows)\\r\\n    files.append(f\\"insert into lr_explanation_choice_notes (explanation_id,letter,body) select v.e,v.l,v.b from (values\\\\n{vals}\\\\n) v(e,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_choice_notes x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.letter=v.l) returning id;\\")\\r\\nfor i, s in enumerate(files, 1):\\r\\n    open(f\\"{prefix}-{i:02d}.sql\\", \'w\', encoding=\'utf-8\').write(s)\\r\\nprint(len(files), [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 12.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"d7c558","wall_time_seconds":0.2677099,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1021,"output":"---\\r\\nname: wrapup\\r\\ndescription: Close an LSAT U session by updating the Obsidian vault so the next chat picks up cleanly. Marks finished tasks done (struck through) in the task ledger, refreshes the handoff cache, and appends to the log. Trigger with /wrapup.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nClose the current LSAT U / Aspiring Attorneys session. Leave the next session a clean, accurate handoff and mark completed work as done so it is never re-run. Resolve the vault root from the current workspace and its nearest instruction files; never assume a saved `~/ai-os/`, Windows, or Mac path. In this vault, read `system/schema/llm-wiki.md` and obey its index/log conventions.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Step 0: read the anchor\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore writing anything, re-read `wiki/tasks.md` and `wiki/hot.md` so the handoff reflects the real goal, not just the last thing touched. If a `plans/` file or README framed this session, skim it too.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Step 1: confirm task status changes only when needed\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom verified session work, determine whether task status changes are needed. If no task status changes are needed, leave `wiki/tasks.md` alone and continue with the authorized handoff and log updates without a confirmation step. If task status changes are proposed, show Joshua the concrete proposal before editing `wiki/tasks.md`:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nProposing for wiki/tasks.md:\\r\\n  -> Done (strike through): <items you believe are finished this session>\\r\\n  -> Stays in Now: <still in progress>\\r\\n  -> New Next: <anything surfaced that should be queued>\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nWait for Joshua to approve or correct proposed task status changes, unless those exact changes were already approved in this session. Completion MUST be correct: a wrong \\"Done\\" is the exact failure this prevents. Do not mark something done on a guess; if unsure, leave its status unchanged and say so. This gate applies to task status changes, not to routine authorized handoff/log filing. If requesting approval, explain that this skill requires confirmation before changing task status.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Step 2: write the authorized handoff and log\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **`wiki/tasks.md`** - move approved finished items into `## Done`, wrapping each in `~~...~~` with a date, e.g. `~~Wire AI tutor into official drill surface~~ (2026-06-12)`. Update `## Now` and `## Next` only as approved. Preserve older task records; do not prune or delete them without explicit authorization. Skip this file when no task changes were approved or needed.\\r\\n2. **`wiki/hot.md`** - rewrite the `## Most recent (YYYY-MM-DD): <title>` block with 4 to 8 bullets of what changed. Refresh `## Active priorities` and `## Open threads` so they are true now.\\r\\n3. **`wiki/log.md`** - append a new entry at the BOTTOM of the log using `## [YYYY-MM-DD] system | Session wrapup`. Include source/output links, meaningful changed pages, verified results, unresolved work, and any skipped steps. Use Joshua\'s client date/time context. Preserve old entries exactly, including their legacy formats; never rewrite or reorder them. Multiple sessions per day are normal, so add a distinct title suffix when useful. If this session\'s authorized handoff and log entry already contain the same verified work, report them rather than duplicating the entry.\\r\\n4. **Task anchor** - if a README, plan, or wiki page advanced, make a surgical status update. Touch only what changed.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Step 3: confirm, then stop\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nHanded off: <1 line headline of this session>\\r\\nTasks: <N marked done, M still in Now>\\r\\nLogged: <the wiki/log.md entry label you added>\\r\\nNext session starts at: <the top open item in tasks.md / hot.md>\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Rules\\r\\n\\r\\n- No em dashes. Terse. Surgical changes only (Karpathy: touch only what changed).\\r\\n- `raw/` is immutable; never write there.\\r\\n- `pickup` is the mirror skill for session start; run it only when explicitly requested.\\r\\n- Read current target contents before writing. Preserve complete ideas, logical strength, modality, and the selected scope.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse original intuitive examples. Explain the ordinary meaning before notation, show the intermediate reasoning, and give questions a real instructional job. Keep the teacher and reader thinking together. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 10.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"f4bb6e","wall_time_seconds":0.3146716,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3499,"output":"(\'PT157-S3-Q25\',\'step\',\'2ff83bdd-29a8-4a08-b301-adfd45fc3305\',\'2\',\'b9aec86e-0805-4623-8fce-3681bdb8332f\',\'e441a94ff321a00e49811abaf4c0d228\',\'Conclusion\',\'We can tentatively conclude that vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing the severity of acne.\',\'Conclusion\',false),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q25\',\'step\',\'2ff83bdd-29a8-4a08-b301-adfd45fc3305\',\'3\',\'5b90d24d-3bc5-4de9-9096-fbd5e6b4df0d\',\'62da77033f804db46c9ac2fe7521dc5e\',\'Evaluate\',\'Let\'\'s be clear about which groups are being compared. Overall, the vitamin C group had less severe acne than the placebo group. Then it turned out that half of each group knew which pill they had, and half could not tell.\\r\\n\\r\\nAmong the subjects who could not tell, there was no difference in acne. So the overall gap came from the subjects who knew. The author\'\'s reading: knowing you got the vitamin made the difference, not the vitamin. The blind comparison is treated as the fair test, and that test found nothing.\\r\\n\\r\\nThat reading depends on the blind comparison really being fair. Finding \\"no difference in the severity of acne\\" means both blind groups ended up in the same place during the study. It only shows the vitamin did nothing if both groups started in the same place too.\\r\\n\\r\\nGiven that, how could we argue vitamin C did help? Suppose the vitamin C subjects started with worse acne. Then ending up equal to the placebo group means they improved more. The \\"no difference\\" result would hide a real benefit.\',\'Evaluate\',true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q25\',\'step\',\'2ff83bdd-29a8-4a08-b301-adfd45fc3305\',\'4\',\'3f4a73c5-614b-4bf2-ab31-81985b672320\',\'b128c72210a1298b2ce9db33f277334e\',\'Goal\',\'Look for an answer showing the blind comparison was not a fair one: most likely, that the vitamin C subjects started out with worse acne than the placebo subjects, so matching them during the study is evidence of improvement. More generally, any answer giving evidence that vitamin C actually helped the blind subjects will weaken.\',\'Goal\',true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q25\',\'note\',\'2ff83bdd-29a8-4a08-b301-adfd45fc3305\',\'A\',\'333af76a-9d2e-463a-89b1-af6de01cb2d9\',\'e994253477cfbf7a900157ef35387c34\',NULL,\'The conclusion rests on the blind comparison showing \\"no difference in the severity of acne\\" between the groups. A says the vitamin C subjects \\"had a history of suffering from more severe acne\\" than the placebo subjects. If the vitamin C group started worse and ended up equal, it improved more than the placebo group did. That is a benefit, hidden inside a result of no difference.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reusable test for study arguments: before trusting a comparison, ask whether the two groups started out alike. If they didn\'\'t, equal results can mean unequal effects.\',NULL,true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q25\',\'note\',\'2ff83bdd-29a8-4a08-b301-adfd45fc3305\',\'B\',\'8746afd0-c226-4735-9ab3-3898726ee7d9\',\'14bff4a517f178c4673e432ac9286875\',NULL,\'B rules out vitamin C subjects taking extra doses on their own. That closes a loophole, but it doesn\'\'t make the blind comparison less trustworthy, and it gives no evidence that the vitamin worked. The no-difference result among blind subjects stands as it was.\',NULL,true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q25\',\'note\',\'2ff83bdd-29a8-4a08-b301-adfd45fc3305\',\'C\',\'b14f1e1a-4a4f-4b57-a126-4cda19b76316\',\'4516a3c5685e6d8bd724186e512da04e\',NULL,\'C describes the subjects as a whole: their acne was below the national average. The argument compares the groups inside the study, so a fact about the whole pool doesn\'\'t change that comparison. C gives no reason to think the vitamin C group would have been worse off without the pills.\',NULL,true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q25\',\'note\',\'2ff83bdd-29a8-4a08-b301-adfd45fc3305\',\'D\',\'011c47e1-9daa-4d67-8a2d-7bff9623600d\',\'7607136305e19c07884097c1e8af9777\',NULL,\'D is the most tempting trap. If placebo subjects got vitamin C from food, the control group was not vitamin-free. But D says only that \\"Some\\" placebo subjects ate vitamin C-rich foods, which is an ordinary diet and could just as easily describe the vitamin C group. Nothing suggests the food matched the pill\'\'s dose or that it happened among the blind subjects. So D barely dents the comparison, while A gives direct evidence that the vitamin helped.\',NULL,true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q25\',\'note\',\'2ff83bdd-29a8-4a08-b301-adfd45fc3305\',\'E\',\'64024504-9bfd-41b6-ba6e-b7c4ad66198d\',\'001a520ec98cdfb416489d1726f37a1b\',NULL,\'Some subjects who knew they had placebos skipped them. Those subjects are in the half that knew, which the conclusion already sets aside. And a placebo has no active ingredient, so taking it or not changes nothing about vitamin C. The blind comparison is untouched.\',NULL,true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q6\',\'step\',\'fd503d16-bf1e-4ec7-82ae-a3d6bfa80905\',\'0\',\'cc53aff6-ee23-421c-b35e-5ba80a773af0\',\'27e8969ac147a22bc90964b7c3ff0c5c\',\'Evidence\',\'Winchester can\'\'t afford to keep its several small branch libraries supplied with a wide selection of current books. The large library could carry broader and more current selections [of books].\',\'Evidence\',true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q6\',\'step\',\'fd503d16-bf1e-4ec7-82ae-a3d6bfa80905\',\'1\',\'68621288-103b-44ab-9e0e-845125bcb6da\',\'4ec38968d2d408f60952a2a791636efb\',\'Conclusion\',\'Winchester would better meet its residents\'\' needs by closing its small branch libraries and opening one larger, well-supplied library.\',\'Conclusion\',false),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q6\',\'step\',\'fd503d16-bf1e-4ec7-82ae-a3d6bfa80905\',\'2\',\'e636f5a1-765a-49c7-994b-f5129fddd592\',\'468b4a271c78c5779c7d51ff9125ef28\',\'Evaluate\',\'The conclusion makes a recommendation about building a new library. It does this by introducing a new concept of better meeting residents\'\' needs. A couple of assumptions are required to reach this conclusion. First, the argument assumes that one large library would better meet residents\'\' needs than several smaller ones. We can easily envision a situation where it\'\'s convenient for residents to visit the branch libraries that are close to their neighborhoods, while a single main library would be farther away and therefore less accessible. The argument also assumes that a broader and more current selection of books would better meet residents\'\' needs. But people use libraries for many reasons other than borrowing books. Libraries often have computers, study rooms, and meeting rooms that residents can use. Libraries often offer classes and events. And people are likely to borrow older books from libraries, not just current ones.\\r\\n\\r\\nSo, given that one big library would have better books, how could we argue residents would be worse off? Show that the new library would fail them in some other way, most likely because they could not easily get to it.\',\'Evaluate\',true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q6\',\'step\',\'fd503d16-bf1e-4ec7-82ae-a3d6bfa80905\',\'3\',\'555b06cd-74bb-4c12-9cd2-e149030e0e28\',\'854311ac18f87a6ddc64d5ad7e322d1a\',\'Goal\',\'A correct answer is likely to focus on one of these unintended consequences of closing the branches, a cost that would stop one large library from serving residents better than the existing branches. The likeliest target: residents could not easily reach the single new library.\',\'Goal\',true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q6\',\'note\',\'fd503d16-bf1e-4ec7-82ae-a3d6bfa80905\',\'A\',\'cc81cecf-f6fe-442b-b254-530fc6c2342c\',\'f9dd130f2f8359b10b5764af288a906e\',NULL,\'Residents who never use the branches because of irregular hours lose nothing when the branches close. If anything, A gives another reason the branches are failing residents. It shows no way the single large library would serve them worse.\',NULL,true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q6\',\'note\',\'fd503d16-bf1e-4ec7-82ae-a3d6bfa80905\',\'B\',\'6d21b69b-2c6f-4d17-aa28-a4bd25c33fcc\',\'0d31d88b459bd5234a5e7c6b5561b02d\',NULL,\'Complaints about the book selection at the branches back up the argument\'\'s premise that the branches are poorly supplied. That makes a bigger, better-stocked library sound more welcome, so B strengthens rather than weakens.\',NULL,true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q6\',\'note\',\'fd503d16-bf1e-4ec7-82ae-a3d6bfa80905\',\'C\',\'dd8204b9-7a09-4650-81d9-f5617f203182\',\'8acd27564c220548d8da6cc771342dd9\',NULL,\'The argument assumes better books means better service. C says \\"The only possible site for a new library is not readily accessible to most Winchester residents.\\" A library most residents can\'\'t easily reach does not meet their needs, however good its books are. The branches, poorly stocked as they are, at least sit nearby.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe move to repeat: when an argument recommends a plan for one benefit, look for the cost it ignored.\',NULL,true),\\r\\n(\'PT157-S3-Q6\',\'note\',\'fd503d16-bf1e-4ec7-82ae-a3d6bfa80905\',\'D\',\'625b0672-92bf-45a7-8d48-e04bb352d8c3\',\'87761a46c8aa1d291620bf3bf3adb433\',NULL,\'The argument already says closing the branches would let Winchester afford the large library.
